Appendix A
Superoperator projectors

This appendix explores the properties of the orthogonal superoperator projectors that are used as the principal tool for the characterization in the main text. These are defined under the notion of a projector on an operator system in Section
A.1
. Six operations whose purpose is motivated by the main text are then defined on this set. These are: the intersection and union,
\cap,\cup
, the negation,
\overline{\>\cdot\>}
, the tensor and transformation,
\otimes,\rightarrow
, and the prec,
\prec
, successively reviewed in Sec.
A.2
,
A.3
,
A.4
, and
A.5
. Some important properties of these operations are proven for the purposes of the main text, among which the fact that each operation applied on commuting projector(s) on operator system(s) results in commuting projector(s) on operator system(s), justifying the assumption of commutation while proving Theorem
1
.

A.1
Definition and required properties of superoperator projectors

Projectors on operator systems.

As explained below Def.
1
, a projector on an operator system
\mathcal{P}_{A}:\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)
is a linear orthogonal superoperator projector to a self-adjoint subspace that contains the identity.

A linear superoperator
\mathcal{P}_{A}
is a
projector
on a subspace of
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)
if and only if it is idempotent,

\mathcal{P}_{A}\circ\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A}\>.

(74)

An operator
V\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)
belongs to the subspace defined by
\mathcal{P}_{A}
if and only if

\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{V\right\}=V\>.

(75)

\mathcal{P}_{A}
projects to a subspace closed under the adjoint if and only if it obeys

\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{V\right\}=V\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{V^{\dagger}\right\}=V^{\dagger}\>,

(76)

which can be written concisely as
\mathcal{P}_{A}\circ{\dagger}={\dagger}\circ\mathcal{P}_{A}
where
{\dagger}
means ‘taking the adjoint in
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)
’.

A projector is
orthogonal
if it does not increase the norm of operators:

\big|\big|\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{V\right\}\big|\big|\leq\big|\big|V\big|\big|\>,

(77)

where
\big|\big|V\big|\big|\equiv\sqrt{\text{Tr}\left[V^{\dagger}\cdot V\right]}
is the Hilbert-Schmidt norm.
Note that this condition is equivalent to requiring the projector to be self-adjoint with respect to the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product
\left\langle V^{\prime}\,,\,V\right\rangle\equiv\text{Tr}\left[V^{{}^{\prime}{\dagger}}\cdot V\right]
(see
e.g.
Ref.
(
Roman 2008
, Theorem 10.5)
),

\text{Tr}\left[\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{V^{\prime}\right\}^{\dagger}\cdot V\right]=\text{Tr}\left[V^{\prime{\dagger}}\cdot\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{V\right\}\right]\>,\quad\forall V,V^{\prime}\>.

(78)

As the projectors appearing in the main text are used to talk about the subspace spanned by a collection of elements rather than the elements themselves, what is truly relevant in the projective characterization is the image of the projectors, not the projectors themselves. Consequently, there is no loss of generality in assuming every projector to be orthogonal since there always exists an orthogonal projector with the same image as any given projector. Having this property will greatly simplify some of the proofs.
For a linear map
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right),\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)\right)
, we note its Hilbert-Schmidt adjoint with a
*
i.e.,
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is the unique map satisfying
\left\langle V^{\prime}\,,\,\mathcal{M}\left(V\right)\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathcal{M}^{*}(V^{\prime})\,,\,V\right\rangle
for all
V,V^{\prime}
. Using this notation, the self-adjointness condition can be written concisely as
\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathcal{P}^{*}_{A}
.

As the projectors should project onto operator systems, which are subspaces that contain the identity, it should be true that their images contain the span of the identity as a subspace. A necessary and sufficient condition for that to be true is

\mathcal{P}_{A}\circ\mathcal{D}_{A}=\mathcal{D}_{A}\circ\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathcal{D}_{A}\>,

(79)

with
\mathcal{D}_{A}
defined as in Eq. (
10
).

Definition and example.

Summarizing, a projector on an operator system is a linear superoperator
\mathcal{P}_{A}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right),\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)\right)
obeying the defining conditions (
74
), (
76
), and (
79
). Moreover, for the purpose of this article, it is assumed to be self-adjoint without loss of generality, condition (
78
).

Putting everything together, the projector appearing in Eq. (
7c
) is an orthogonal projector on an operator system, meaning it obeys the following set of conditions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}\circ\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A}\>;

(80a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}\circ{\dagger}={\dagger}\circ\mathcal{P}_{A}\>;

(80b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{*}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A}\>;

(80c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}\circ\mathcal{D}_{A}=\mathcal{D}_{A}\circ\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathcal{D}_{A}\>.

(80d)

When referring to ‘a projector’ in this article, we implicitly mean ‘an orthogonal projector on an operator system’, i.e., ‘a projector obeying conditions (
80
)’, unless said otherwise.

Examples of projectors on operator systems over a 2-dimensional Hilbert space can be generated using the Pauli basis
\{\mathds{1},\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y},\sigma_{z}\}
(see e.g., Ref.
Nielsen and Chuang 2009
for its definition and properties): it can be checked that any projector ‘removing’ one or several basis elements other than the identity is a valid projector on an operator system i.e., obeys (
80
).
Such is the case of the projector to the subspace of operators that are diagonal in the computational basis, which is used for quantum decoherence. This projector, noted
\Delta:\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)
and which acts as
\Delta(\cdot)\equiv\frac{\mathds{1}}{2}\text{Tr}\left[\cdot\right]+\frac{\sigma_{z}}{2}\text{Tr}\left[\sigma_{z}\>\cdot\right]
, is the projector to the subspace spanned by
\{\mathds{1},\sigma_{z}\}
, thus a projector on an operator system.

Sets of commuting base projectors.

For the purposes of this work, it is assumed that all the projectors under consideration always commute pairwise when acting on the same tensor factor of a Hilbert space.
Remark that the commutation of two projectors is a sufficient condition for their composition to be a projector as well (see e.g., Ref.
(
Roman 2008
, Theorem 2.26 3))
).

At first glance, the physical heuristic behind the assumption is that we are only interested in building (higher-order) maps between subsystems of a fixed kind: if Alice’s system is a qubit and Bob’s is a classical bit, this will not change whether we consider their joint state or a channel between them; the framework developed in this article is only used to characterize their joint state space or the set of channels they can share, not what happens when Alice suddenly demotes her system to a bit. The characterization is done by combining the base projectors of Alice and Bob, which in this case are
\mathcal{I}_{A}
and
\Delta_{B}
(where
\mathcal{I}
is the identity projector and
\Delta
is the projector to the diagonal subspace as defined above). For that reason, it can be assumed that there is only one base projector associated with each factor of a Hilbert space at the start of the construction.

The rules introduced in the main text and detailed below then lead to several ways to combine the projectors of Alice and Bob into a projector acting on space
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B}\right)
. Hence, the ‘Alice+Bob’ system can be of several different natures: it can be bipartite states, but also channels (in CJ representation), bipartite measurement, etc. All of these correspond to different projectors, and each of these projectors can in turn be seen as the base projectors in the coarse-grained, ‘Alice+Bob’ description. One of the things shown in this appendix is precisely that every introduced rule to build new projectors out of known ones will preserve commutation. Hence, any two projectors characterizing systems of a different type and obtained by relating the same base projectors on the same subsystems using any of the various algebraic rules defined in the following will commute by construction.

Non-singleton set of base projectors.

Upon closer inspection, it may still be interesting to study what happens when the base subsystems can be picked among a set of possible types. For instance, it is very natural to extrapolate the characterization of a class of processes to a case in which, say, Alice’s state preparation becomes a discarding procedure (graphically, it amounts to changing Alice’s wire from facing up to facing down). This replacement actually corresponds to the ‘not’ operation in the algebra, so it will preserve commutation as will be proven below. Hence, any base projector can be associated with a second base projector, its negation. In addition, the operation of ‘intersection’ and ‘union’ will also result in two new projectors that commute with both the base projector and its negation. Therefore, even when one considers one base projector per subsystem, it is always extensible to a set of at least four pairwise commuting base projectors.
There is no point in restricting the set of base projectors then and one can consider any commuting set of base projectors in general.
Therefore, for the projective characterization developed in this work, the general setting considers
k
parties
A,B,...
, each of which having access to
n_{A},n_{B},...
base projectors
\{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}_{A},\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{A},...,\mathcal{P}^{(n_{A})}_{A}\}
,
\{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}_{B},\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{B},...,\mathcal{P}^{(n_{B})}_{B}\}
, … corresponding to the different types of subsystems they may have access to. The commutation assumption, which can be expressed as

\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X}\circ\mathcal{P}^{(j)}_{X}=\mathcal{P}^{(j)}_{X}\circ\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X}\>,\quad\forall X,\,\forall i,j\>.

(81)

As mentioned in the related comment made at the end of Sec.
V.4
, these will not only encompass all higher-order objects that could have been built using a fine-graining of the subsystem. For example, it can also include scenarios in which Alice can choose whether she wants to use a classical or quantum bit as her system to model scenarios featuring potential decoherence. In our framework, this corresponds to Alice having a set of base projectors made of two elements,
\{\mathcal{I}_{A},\Delta_{A}\}
(which will then be extended to a set of eight elements by using the not, intersection and union operations). We want to bring to the reader’s attention that since these two projectors commute, all the results of this paper will still hold, albeit it is impossible to build
\Delta
out of
\mathcal{I}
using any of the rules presented in this article. This is thus a situation characterized by our result but not considered in this article, which opens a potential direction for future research.

Commutation with the transposition.

A follow-up consequence of assuming that all projectors commute pairwise is that, when acting on self-adjoint operators, they can be assumed to commute with the transposition induced by the CJ isomorphism. This condition will be required simply because it removes the necessity of keeping track of the transposes, resulting in less clustered formulae.

Here, transposition is meant in a more general sense than matrix transpose. The CJ correspondence depends on a choice of basis, namely the one in which the matrix transpose that appears in Eq. (
1
) is performed. This implicitly means that the standard bases of
\mathcal{H}^{A}
and
\mathcal{H}^{B}
were chosen to define the isomorphism. However, this is an arbitrary choice. Eq. (
1
) is a shortcut for

\displaystyle M=

(82)

\displaystyle\text{Tr}_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\left[(\phi^{+}_{AA^{\prime}}\otimes\phi^{+}_{B^{\prime}B})\big(\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes(\mathcal{I}\otimes\mathcal{M})\{\phi^{+}\}_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\big)\right],

and it is only because
\phi^{+}
was chosen to be
\phi^{+}=\sum_{i,j}\ket{i}\bra{j}\otimes\ket{i}\bra{j}
that it corresponds to matrix transposition in the standard basis.
Yet, any other choice of unnormalised maximally entangled density matrix
\Phi
is equally good. Let
\{e_{\mu}\}_{\mu=0}^{d_{A}^{2}-1}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)
. Then this basis can always be associated with an unnormalized maximally entangled state of the form
\Phi_{A^{\prime}A}=\sum_{\mu}e_{\mu}\otimes e_{\mu}
, which is in turn used to define a transposition with respect to
\{e_{\mu}\}
by

V_{A}^{T}\equiv\text{Tr}_{A^{\prime}}\left[\Phi\>(V_{A^{\prime}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{A})\right],\quad\forall V_{A^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}}\right)\>.

(83)

This abstract notion of transposition happens to correspond to the one of matrix transpose with respect to a basis
\{\ket{\varphi_i}\}_{i=0}^{d_{A}-1}
of
\mathcal{H}^{A}
precisely when the basis
\{e_{\mu}\}
is
\{e_{\mu}:=\ket{\varphi_i}\bra{\varphi_j}\}_{\mu=(i,j)}
, but it does not have to have this form to define a CJ correspondence. All that matters is that a pair of bases for
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)
and
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{B}\right)
is chosen.

This freedom of basis in the definition of the CJ correspondence can be leveraged to make all projectors commute with the transpositions it induces. For instance, this work typically deals with equations of the form

\mathcal{M}(V_{A})=\mathcal{P}_{B}\left\{\mathcal{M}\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{V_{A}\right\}\right)\right\}\>,

(84)

where
\mathcal{M}
is CP map,
V_{A}
a positive operator, and
\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\mathcal{P}_{B}
are projectors on operator systems.
Our methods will use the reverse direction of the CJ correspondence (
2
) to rephrase the above into

\mathcal{M}(V_{A})=\mathcal{P}_{B}\left\{\big(\text{Tr}_{A}\left[M\>(\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{V_{A}\right\}\otimes\mathds{1})\right]\big)^{T}\right\}\>,

(85)

and then gather all projectors into a single expression as in

\mathcal{M}(V_{A})=\big(\text{Tr}_{A}\left[((\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B})\{M\})\>(V_{A}\otimes\mathds{1})\right]\big)^{T}\>,

(86)

where the tensor product of projectors is defined in a standard way (see Eq. (
17
) or in App.
A.4.1
below). Going from Eq. (
85
) to (
86
) assumed that
\big(\mathcal{P}_{B}\left\{N_{B}\right\}\big)^{T}=\mathcal{P}_{B}\left\{N_{B}^{T}\right\}
for all
N_{B}
. A similar rewriting can also be made when computing
\mathcal{M}^{*}(N_{B})
, in which case one needs to assume that
\big(\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{V_{A}\right\}\big)^{T}=\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{V_{A}^{T}\right\}
for all
V_{A}
. When all operators are self-adjoint, we now show that these assumptions can always be satisfied by picking a specific choice of bases to define the CJ isomorphism.

The general statement we prove is that if the base projectors form a set of
n_{X}
pairwise commuting orthogonal projectors on operator systems over subsystem
X
,
\{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}_{X},\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{X},\mathcal{P}^{(3)}_{X},\ldots,\mathcal{P}^{(n_{X})}_{X}\}
, the following can be ensured:

\big(\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X}\left\{V\right\}\big)^{T}=\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X}\left\{V^{T}\right\}\>,\quad\forall i,\>\forall V:\>V=V^{\dagger}\>,

(87)

for the transpositions induced by the CJ isomorphism. This is done by choosing the basis for the isomorphism to be the one diagonalizing the set of projectors.

If this basis is chosen to be the one that diagonalizes the set of base projectors, i.e. if the base projectors is a set of
n
pairwise commuting orthogonal projectors
\{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}_{X},\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{X},\mathcal{P}^{(3)}_{X},\ldots,\mathcal{P}^{(n_{X})}_{X}\}
expressed in this basis as
\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X}\left\{\cdot\right\}=\sum_{\mu}c^{(i)}_{\mu}\>e_{\mu}\text{Tr}\left[e_{\mu}^{\dagger}\>\cdot\right]
with
i=1,\ldots,n_{X}
and
c^{(i)}_{\mu}\in\{0,1\}
, then

\sum_{\nu}\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X^{\prime}}\left\{e_{\nu}\right\}\otimes e_{\nu}=\sum_{\nu}e_{\nu}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X}\left\{e_{\nu}\right\}\>,

(88)

and it follows that the projectors commute with the transpose when applied on self-adjoint operators. Indeed,
\forall V:\>V=V^{\dagger}
and
\forall i
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X}\left\{V^{T}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{A}\left\{\text{Tr}_{X^{\prime}}\left[(V_{X^{\prime}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{X})(\sum_{\nu}e_{\nu}\otimes e_{\nu})\right]\right\}

(89)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}_{X^{\prime}}\left[(V_{X^{\prime}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{X})(\sum_{\nu}e_{\nu}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X}\left\{e_{\nu}\right\})\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}_{X^{\prime}}\left[(V_{X^{\prime}}\otimes\mathds{1}_{X})(\sum_{\nu}\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X^{\prime}}\left\{e_{\nu}\right\}\otimes e_{\nu})\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}_{A^{\prime}}\left[(\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X^{\prime}}\left\{V_{X^{\prime}}\right\}\otimes\mathds{1}_{X})(\sum_{\nu}e_{\nu}\otimes e_{\nu})\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X}\left\{V\right\}\right)^{T}\>,

where passing from the third to the fourth line was possible only because
V=V^{\dagger}
and
\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X}
is self-adjoint by definition.

Definition.

Summarizing, any subsystem
X
will be associated with
n_{X}
different types given a priori, called their base types. These base types correspond to
n_{X}
different state structures to which are associated
n_{X}
base projectors noted
\{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}_{X},\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{X},\mathcal{P}^{(3)}_{X},\ldots,\mathcal{P}^{(n_{X})}_{X}\}
or
\{\mathcal{P}_{X},\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{X},\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{X},\ldots\}
. For every self-adjoint operator
V\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{X}\right)
, the following is always assumed to hold for a set of base projectors:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X}\circ\mathcal{P}^{(j)}_{X}=\mathcal{P}^{(j)}_{X}\circ\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X}\>,\quad\forall i,j\>;

(90a)

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X}\left\{V\right\}\big)^{T}=\mathcal{P}^{(i)}_{X}\left\{V^{T}\right\}\>,\quad\forall i\>.

(90b)

Remark: non-pairwise-commuting set of base projectors.

The assumption that the base projectors all commute is reasonable for the scope of this paper and potential extensions, as argued in the above discussion. Yet it is not necessary: it was only made because there are no heuristical reasons to go beyond it. While it greatly simplifies the mathematics by allowing the intersection (or cap) operation
\cap
to be defined as the composition of two projectors (Eq. (
22
) in the main text; its mathematical properties are studied in the next subsection below), dropping it may also open a direction for further research: the natural follow-up question to considering a higher-order theory based on a set of pairwise commuting base projectors would be to study what happens when the base projectors are no longer commuting. In this case, most of the results would have to be generalized in a version that does not use this assumption nor the related one that the projectors commute with the transpose. Nonetheless, besides pure mathematical curiosity, we do not have a clear idea of what the purpose of such a framework may be; let alone its physical interpretation.

A.2
Adding cap and cup operations makes an algebra

Here, we prove that superoperator projectors on operator systems constitute an algebra under certain rules. Assume
\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}
to be two arbitrary projectors on operator systems, not necessarily the same. By definition, a projector is
\mathbb{C}
-linear:

\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{\sum_{i}q_{i}V_{i}\right\}=\sum_{i}\;q_{i}\;\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{V_{i}\right\}\>,

(91)

q_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
.
This linearity allows us to carry on the addition ‘+’ of
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)
at the level of the projectors:

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right)\{V\}\equiv\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{V\right\}+\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\left\{V\right\}\>.

(92)

Using linearity again, one can define the negation ‘-’ and scalar multiplication of projectors, thereby defining a vector space over
\mathbb{C}
. Associativity and commutativity are not hard to prove from there.

Projectors also have a natural ‘conjunction’ operation that can be interpreted as a multiplication or as a logic ‘and’, here nicknamed the
intersection
or the
cap
and noted via the ‘
\cap
’ symbol. In the case of commuting projectors
\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}
, it is

\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\equiv\mathcal{P}_{A}\circ\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\>.

(93)

This projector
\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}
characterizes the intersection of the operator systems
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
, see the main text.
It is straightforward to prove that it fits the definition of a conjunction: it is a binary operation distributive under the addition defined above and compatible with the scalar multiplication; it is both associative and commutative; for projectors verifying Eqs. (
90
), it is resulting in a projector verifying Eqs. (
90
); and it is idempotent when acting on projectors since:

\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}_{A}\equiv\mathcal{P}_{A}\circ\mathcal{P}_{A}\equiv\mathcal{P}^{2}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A}\>.

(94)

In the above equation, we have also defined a shorthand notation for ‘squaring’ under the multiplication
\cap
:
\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}_{A}\equiv\mathcal{P}^{2}_{A}
.

An issue revealed by squaring is that the operation ‘+’ does not necessarily map projectors to a valid projector:

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right)^{2}=\mathcal{P}^{2}_{A}+\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}2}_{A}+2\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right)\>.

(95)

As the last term of the right-hand side is not always zero,
\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right)^{2}\neq\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}
in general, so addition does not preserve idempotency. We wish nonetheless to have operations that keep us in the vector space of commuting projectors, so we redefine the addition to be the union of two projectors (see
e.g.
Ref.
Piziak et al. 1999
). This is inspired by set theory: as
\mathcal{P}_{A}
maps to a subspace
\mathscr{A}\subset\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)
and
\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}
to
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)
we want an addition ‘
\cup
’ so that
\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}
maps to
\mathscr{A}\cup\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
. This requirement is realized by the ‘disjunction’ operation, that can be interpreted as an addition or as logic ‘or’, nicknamed the
union
or the
cup
(see also Ref.
Hoffreumon and Oreshkov 2021
):

\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\equiv\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\>.

(96)

As the name hints,
\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}
characterizes the union of the operator systems
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
. It is again straightforward to prove that it fits the definition from the properties inherited by the addition and the cap: the cup is a binary operation distributive under the addition defined above and compatible with the scalar multiplication; it is both associative and commutative; for projectors verifying Eqs. (
90
) it is resulting in a projector verifying Eqs. (
90
); and it inherits the idempotency from the idempotency of the cap as
\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{P}_{A}-(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}_{A})=\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{P}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}
.

These caps and cup connectors give a quick way to prove subspace inclusions. The subspace spanned by state structure
\mathscr{A}^{\prime}
is embedded in the subspace spanned by another state structure
\mathscr{A}
if and only if either of the following holds (see e.g., Ref.
Roman 2008
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}=\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\>;

(97a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A}\>.

(97b)

The intersection and union of two projectors project to, respectively, the intersection and union of their images
(
Roman 2008
, Theorem 2.26)
i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\left\{\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right\}=\mathrm{Im}\left\{\mathcal{P}_{A}\right\}\cap\mathrm{Im}\left\{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right\}\>,

(98a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\left\{\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right\}=\mathrm{Im}\left\{\mathcal{P}_{A}\right\}\cup\mathrm{Im}\left\{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right\}\>,

(98b)

where the
\cap
and
\cup
appearing in the right-hand side of the above are the set-theoretic notions of intersection and union, respectively.
Condition (
80d
) is then recast into

\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{D}_{A}=\mathcal{D}_{A}\>.

(99)

As defined in Eq. (
25
) of the main text, obeying Eqs. (
97
) is concisely noted as
\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{A}
and means that such an equation must be understood in terms of the image of the projectors. Accordingly, an equivalence of two projectors is defined as the equivalence of their images:

\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\iff\Im{\Proj{}{A}}=\Im{\Proj{'}{A}}\>.

(100)

The algebra.

Commuting projectors on operator systems thus form an algebra over
\mathbb{C}
since they are closed under
(\cap,\cup)
operations as well as scalar multiplication.
The cap and cup can indeed be seen as some multiplication and addition of projectors as the former distributes over the latter:

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right)\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{A}=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{A}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{A}\right)\>.

(101)

Indeed,
\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right)\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{A}=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}-(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A})\right)\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{A}=\big(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{A}\big)+\big(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{A}\big)-\big(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\big)\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{A}
, and
\big(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\big)\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{A}=\big(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\big)\cap\big(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{A}\big)=\big(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{A}\big)\cap\big(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{A}\big)
.

The projectors
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{D}
defined in Eqs. (
8
) and (
10
) above play a special role in it: one can identify the identity superoperator
\mathcal{I}
as the multiplicative identity or ‘
unit
’ of the algebra of projectors since for all projectors
\mathcal{P}
,

\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}\>;

(102)

it is also the additive absorbant i.e.

\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{I}\>.

(103)

The other way around, the depolarizing superoperator
\mathcal{D}
is the multiplicative absorbant or ‘
zero
’ of the algebra,

\mathcal{D}\cap\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{D}\>,

(104)

and the additive identity,

\mathcal{D}\cup\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}\>.

(105)

Because of that property, it should be clear that for any element of the algebra, the following is true:

\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\>.

(106)

As a consequence, this is an algebra of idempotent elements equipped with a partial ordering
\subseteq
that have a common greatest element
\mathcal{I}
and a common least element
\mathcal{D}
.
Moreover, the intersection and union of any two elements
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}
are uniquely defined elements of the algebra. It is direct to check that the following property holds

\mathcal{P}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}\>,

(107)

and so does the analog with
\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}
. In other words, the algebra has a lattice structure (which directly comes from the lattice structure of subspaces inclusions; see e.g., Ref.
Roman 2008
).

Remark.

Although referring to the cap and cup as ‘multiplication’ and ‘addition’ provides an intuitive meaning to the connectors, in the following we will call them respectively ‘additive disjunction and conjunction’. This choice is made in order to avoid ambiguity with the multiplicative conjunction and disjunction that will be introduced in a next section.

A.3
Adding negation makes a Boolean algebra

In the algebra of superoperator projectors, the new object that naturally appears in Proposition
1
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}
, can be seen as an operation on the original projector
\mathcal{P}_{A}
, whence the ‘bar over
\mathcal{P}_{A}
’ notation. This new operation is defined for any projector, and it promotes the algebra to a
Boolean algebra
.

It is a Boolean algebra if, in addition to the conjunction (‘multiplication’) operation
\cap
and the disjunction (‘addition’) operation
\cup
, any element is idempotent and has a well-defined complement (or logic ‘not’, thereby referred to as ‘negation’
15
15

15

We could also have used the terminology ‘inverse’, ‘dual’, or we could have stuck with ‘complement’, but these words are already used for different concepts in quantum information and functional analysis. This choice is made by consequence to avoid ambiguities.
) characterized by the condition that the addition of any projector with its negation yields the additive identity, i.e.

\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}=\mathcal{I}_{A}\>,

(108)

where
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}
is the negation of
\mathcal{P}_{A}
. This is exactly what the quasi-orthogonal complement of Proposition
1
is doing. First, compute the cap as
\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A}\circ\left(\mathcal{I}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\right)=\mathcal{P}_{A}-\mathcal{P}^{2}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}
, so

\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}=\mathcal{D}_{A}\>.

(109)

Then, it is true for the cup because
\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{I}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}-(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A})=\mathcal{I}_{A}
.
Thus, we define the
negation
of any projector
\mathcal{P}_{A}
as

\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\equiv\mathcal{I}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\>.

(110)

In addition to property (
108
), one can verify from Eq. (
109
) that the projector and its negation intersect at the zero of the algebra, which in this case is indeed
\mathcal{D}_{A}
(refer to the diagram in Fig.
2
).
As it should be, negation is an involution:

\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A}\>,

(111)

and it is closed so that the negation of any projector is a valid projector as we now prove.

Negation yields a valid projector on operator system:

Note that since
\mathcal{I}_{A}
and
\mathcal{D}_{A}
are Hermitian-preserving (HP) self-adjoint (SA), and commute with the transpose (CT), Eqs. (
80b
), (
80c
), and (
90b
), the negation of a projector is HP, SA, and CT provided the original projector is.
The idempotency property (
80a
) is preserved,
\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{2}_{A}=\left(\mathcal{I}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{I}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\right)=\mathcal{I}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{P}^{2}_{A}-\mathcal{D}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}-\mathcal{D}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}=\mathcal{I}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}
, hence

\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{2}_{A}=\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\>.

(112)

To prove that the negation of a projector on an operator system is a projector on an operator system itself it remains to show that the identity is still contained in the negation, Eq. (
80d
),

\mathcal{D}_{A}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}=\mathcal{D}_{A}\>.

(113)

This follows from
\mathcal{D}_{A}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}=\mathcal{D}_{A}\cap\left(\mathcal{I}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\right)=\mathcal{D}_{A}-\mathcal{D}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}
.
If we did not have the heuristic of Proposition
1
, this could be interpreted as a reason for choosing Eq. (
110
) as the definition of negation instead of the orthogonal complement
\mathcal{I}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}
. In this latter case, identity does not belong to the negated state structure.

Negation preserves commutativity:

It should also be clear from
\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\left(\mathcal{I}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\right)=\mathcal{P}_{A}-\mathcal{P}^{2}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}=\left(\mathcal{I}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\right)\cap\mathcal{P}_{A}
that negated projectors commute with the original ones, and, by the same kind of proof, that the negations of two commuting projectors still commute:

\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}=\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}_{A}\Longrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\>.

(114)

De Morgan duality:

Moreover, the De Morgan laws are valid for the Boolean algebra of projectors:

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\>,

(115a)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}}\cup\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\>.

(115b)

The proof is more involved:
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}=\mathcal{I}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}=\mathcal{I}_{A}-(\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}-(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}))+\mathcal{D}_{A}=\mathcal{I}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}-\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}-\mathcal{D}_{A}+(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A})-\mathcal{D}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A}=(\mathcal{I}_{A}-\mathcal{P}_{A}+D)\cap(\mathcal{I}_{A}-\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}+\mathcal{D}_{A})=\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}
. The second identity directly ensues.
The inclusion relation
\subseteq
, defined by conditions (
24
), is by consequence reversed when the projectors it involves are negated. Indeed, if
\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{A}\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}
, negating both sides of the cap yields
\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}=\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}_{A}}
. But the term under the negation in
\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}_{A}}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{P}_{A}
since the inclusion also implies that
\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{A}\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A}
. Whence,
\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}=\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}_{A}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}
, which by definition is the inclusion
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}
. This reasoning works in both ways, proving

\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{A}\>\iff\>\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\>,

(116)

which is equation (
28
) in the main text.

A.4
Adding tensor and transformation almost makes Linear Logic

A.4.1
The tensor operation

For Definition
4
, we also defined a tensor product at the level of the projectors. Here we now present its algebraic properties so that we can meaningfully consider
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}\subset\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B}\right)
when we know that
\mathscr{A}
is characterized by a projector
\mathcal{P}_{A}
acting on
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)
and that
\mathscr{B}
is by
\mathcal{P}_{B}
. Because of the isomorphism
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B}\right)\cong\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)\otimes\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{B}\right)
, the definition of
no signaling composition
, nicknamed
tensor
, is straightforward:

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\left\{\sum_{i}\;q_{i}\;\left(V_{i}\otimes U_{i}\right)\right\}\equiv\\
\sum_{i}\;q_{i}\;\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{V_{i}\right\}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\left\{U_{i}\right\}\right)\>,

(117)

and it should hold for all
i
such that
q_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
,
V_{i}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)
,
U_{i}\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{B}\right)
as any operator in
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B}\right)
can be expressed as such.

By the inherited properties of the tensor product at the level of operators, the tensor at the level of superoperators is associative; it is moreover distributive with respect to the cap,

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right)\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\>,

(118)

as well as to the cup,

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right)\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\>.

(119)

This directly follows from idempotency:

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right)\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right)\otimes\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}_{B}\right),

(120)

and because the intersection of projectors is party-wise, thus it commutes with the tensor product. Another way to see it is that composition of superoperators obeys an interchange law with the tensor product of superoperators:

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right)\otimes\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)\>.

(121)

Tensor yields a valid projector on operator system:

Expressions built from the tensor product of Hermitian-preserving projectors are automatically HP since the dagger distributes over the tensor,
\left(\rho\otimes\sigma\right)^{\dagger}=\rho^{\dagger}\otimes\sigma^{\dagger}
. If the composed projectors are self-adjoint then so is their tensor product since the inner product is
\mathbb{C}
-linear and splits as
\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{\rho_{A}\right\}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\left\{\sigma_{B}\right\}\,,\,\eta_{A}\otimes\chi_{B}\right\rangle_{AB}=\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{A}\left\{\rho_{A}\right\}\,,\,\eta_{A}\right\rangle_{A}\left\langle\mathcal{P}_{B}\left\{\sigma_{B}\right\}\,,\,\chi_{B}\right\rangle_{B}
so that
\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)^{*}=\mathcal{P}^{*}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{*}_{B}
.
They preserve idempotency,

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)^{2}=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:interchange_tensor_cap}}{=}\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}_{A}\right)\otimes\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\>,

(122)

because of interchange law, thus

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)^{2}=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\>.

(123)

A further consequence of the interchange law is that the tensor composition of, respectively, the units and the zeroes on
A
and
B
,
\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}
and
\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}
, respectively yield the unit and zero of the projector algebra on
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B}\right)
,
\mathcal{I}_{AB}
and
\mathcal{D}_{AB}
. Indeed, they can be used to define the negation of
\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}}
, and this gives the correct Boolean completions:
\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\overline{\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)}=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}-\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\right)=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}-\mathcal{P}^{2}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{2}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\right)=\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}
(at the second equality sign, the interchange law was used to compute the cap of
\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}
with each of the three components of the negation).
\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cup\overline{\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)}=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}-\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\right)=\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}+\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}-\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}-\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}=\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\overline{\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)}=\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\equiv\mathcal{D}_{AB}\>;

(124a)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cup\overline{\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)}=\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}\equiv\mathcal{I}_{AB}\>.

(124b)

Tensor composition then “preserves the identity” simply because the identity on a joint system is the tensor product of the identities on each of the spaces being combined,
\mathds{1}_{AB}=\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}
. In again because of the interchange law,
\mathcal{D}_{AB}\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)=\left(\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)=\left(\mathcal{D}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}_{A}\right)\otimes\left(\mathcal{D}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)=\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}
,

\mathcal{D}_{AB}\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)=\mathcal{D}_{AB}\>.

(125)

This shows that the tensor product of two projectors verifying Eqs. (
80
) verifies them as well.

Tensor product preserves commutation:

Since, if
\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}=\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\mathcal{P}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}=\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}_{B}
, then
\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right)\otimes\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)=\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}_{A}\right)\otimes\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)
, and so

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)=\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\>.

(126)

Negation and tensor:

Interestingly, however, the tensor and negation do not commute with each other:
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}}\neq\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}_{B}}
. Actually, the latter expression defines a subspace in the former since

\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}_{B}}=\\
\left(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}-\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\right)\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}_{B}}=\\
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}_{B}}-\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\>,

(127)

so that

\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}_{B}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}_{B}}\>.

(128)

And from this result

\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}}\cup\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}_{B}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}}\>.

(129)

Hence,

\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}_{B}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}}\>;

(130)

(recall that the subset symbol,
\subseteq
, implicitly means that we are considering the images of these projectors). This identity which, using (
116
), can be recast as

\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\subseteq\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}\>,

(131)

is Eq. (
33
) in the main text. That these two projectors are not equal is fundamentally the reason why an indefinite causal order may arise; this will become clearer after the prec is introduced in the algebra so that equations (
161
) and (
162
) can be written down.
For an illustration of this relation, the left-hand side of this identity is depicted in the leftmost diagram in Fig.
9
, whereas the right-hand side is the rightmost.

Because of the interchange law (
121
), relation (
130
) is stable when composed with more parties. More generally, the inclusion relation
\subseteq
induced by conditions (
25
) is stable under the tensor; if
\mathcal{P}_{A}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}
, then the following hold

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\>,

(132a)

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}\subseteq\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}\>,

(132b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\subseteq\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}\>.

(132c)

The last equation is a consequence of the first using (
130
). The first equation holds because

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\overset{\eqref{eq:cap_tenor_dist}}{=}\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right)\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\>,

(133)

and the term in parenthesis is equal to
\mathcal{P}_{A}
because
\mathcal{P}_{A}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}
, therefore the first equation holds. The second equation is proven using De Morgan duality:

\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}\cap\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}\overset{\eqref{eq:deMorgan_cup_to_cap}}{=}\overline{\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}\right)\cup\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}\right)}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:cup_tensor_dist}}{=}\overline{\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\cup\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\right)\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:deMorgan_cap_to_cup}}{=}\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}_{A}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}\\
=\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}\>.

(134)

Relations (
130
) and (
132
) are important to define the no signaling subset of an arbitrary projector, as explained in section
IV.2
.

Finally, observe that the only time the identity
\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}=\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}
holds is when the projector is either characterizing the totality of the space or only the span of identity. In equation,

\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}=\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}\>\iff\>\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{B}=\mathcal{I}\>\text{or}\>\mathcal{D}\>.

(135)

This is proven by rewriting
\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}
into

\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}=\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\>+\\
\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}-\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}-\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\right)+\\
\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}-\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}-\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\right)\>,

(136)

using the definition of negation and algebraic properties. It can then be understood from Fig.
9
: the first term of the above is the right quarter of the wheel, the second is the top quarter with its boundary removed, and the third is the bottom also without boundaries. As the three parts share no intersection, the regular addition ‘+’ is equivalent to a conjunction ‘
\cup
’.
Next, more algebraic manipulations lead to

\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}=\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\>+\\
\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}+\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}-\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}-\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}\right)\>,

(137)

and from this expression it is direct to check that the term in parenthesis vanishes if and only if either of the conditions in Eq. (
135
) hold.

A.4.2
The transformation operation

The projector appearing in Proposition
2
is built from the projectors characterizing its input and output. The corresponding operation is defined as the
transformation
in Eq. (
53
), represented by
\rightarrow
:

\mathcal{P}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\equiv\\
\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}-\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}+\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}-\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\>.

(138)

This additional operation in the Boolean algebra of projectors is actually secondary since it can be entirely defined using the negation and the no signaling composition (i.e., the tensor):

\mathcal{P}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\equiv\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}\>.

(139)

Therefore it will automatically be a valid projector in the sense that it will obey Eqs. (
80
) if its constituents do, and will accordingly preserve commutation if they do.
Furthermore, as this expression involves both composition and negation, it will not be associative in general. For example,
\left(\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{C}\right)\neq\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\rightarrow\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{C}\right)\right)
, because the
uncurrying rule
proven in Ref.
Perinotti 2017
can be applied to the right-hand side:

\mathcal{P}_{A}\rightarrow\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{C}\right)=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{C}\>,

(140)

which is obviously different than
\left(\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{C}\right)
by Eq. (
131
). In the algebra, the uncurrying rule relies on the associativity of the tensor:

\mathcal{P}_{A}\rightarrow\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{C}\right)=\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\overline{\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{B}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{C}}\right)}}\\
=\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}_{B}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{C}}}}\overset{\eqref{eq:invol}}{=}\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{C}}\\
=\overline{\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{C}}=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{C}\>.

(141)

Remark that because of Eq. (
132b
), the transformation also preserves the inclusion relations:

\mathcal{P}_{A}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\>\Rightarrow\>\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\subseteq\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\>.

(142)

At the level of Boolean logic, the transformation operation can be understood as a logical implication. Indeed, the transformation is equal to its inverse implication, meaning that it satisfies

\mathcal{P}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}=\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}}\leftarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{B}}\>,

(143)

which comes from the definition. Additionally, note that it is equivalent to
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{B}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}}
since the order of the systems in the tensor product does not matter as
\mathcal{H}^{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B}\cong\mathcal{H}^{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A}
, we only keep it fixed to avoid adding confusion.

The
\rightarrow
operation on projectors recovers the
\rightarrow
type constructor of Bisio and Perinotti type theory
Perinotti 2017
;
Bisio and Perinotti 2019
(see Sec.
II
): if we define the trivial system as ‘1’, that is to say, the 1-dimensional state structure {1} made of the number 1, one can interpret the measurement (thus the negation of a given state structure) as a transformation into the trivial system. This leads to the identity

\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}}=\mathcal{P}_{A}\rightarrow 1\>,

(144)

which justifies the notation
\mathscr{A}\rightarrow 1\equiv\overline{\mathscr{A}}
. The proof is straightforward from the definition since
1
is 1-dimensional:
\mathcal{P}_{A}\rightarrow 1=\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\overline{1}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}}
. In the same way, one can prove that

\mathcal{P}_{A}=1\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{A}\>.

(145)

Therefore, a state structure can be seen as a transformation from the trivial system to itself and its negation as a transformation from itself to the trivial system.
In view of the link between composition and transformation, one may also interpret the former in terms of the latter. A bipartite system in tensor-composed state structures,
\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{B}
for instance, can actually be seen as characterized by the following transformations

\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}=\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}=\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}}\>.

(146)

This means that a no signaling composite bipartite system is equivalent to a functional on a transformation from one state structure to the functionals on the other of the other as explained in Sec.
II
. In the above equation, we see that the direction of the transformation has no influence, which is expected since it is a no signaling composition; this interpretation is explained in Sec.
V
.

A.4.3
Linear Logic

We now show under which definition that the rules
\{\overline{\>\cdot\>},\cap,\cup,\otimes,\overline{\>\cdot\>}\rightarrow\>\cdot\>\}
form a model of logic which is almost multiplicative additive linear logic (MALL). The correspondence with MALL is summarized in Table
2
, where the rules we have introduced are put in correspondence with their usual notation.

Name

Symbol

Unit

proj.

LL

proj.

LL

Negation

\overline{\>\cdot\>}

\cdot^{\perp}

/

/

Additive conjunction

\cap

\&

\mathcal{I}

Table 2:
Correspondence of the algebraic rules of the projective characterization (proj.) with linear logic (LL)

Linear logic is a formal system of logic, of which MALL is a fragment, that is a restriction of the logic to fewer rules. It can be defined as a
sequent calculus
, that is the formalization of proof systems in which each
proposition
follows under some
structural and inference rules
from other propositions (it is a
sequent
of these propositions, whence the name) see Ref.
Girard 1987
.
If one sees the projectors algebra and the operations on it as the propositions, the starting set of propositions is indeed similar to those of MALL:

1.

The set from which we start is made of valid projectors;

2.

For every projector
\mathcal{P}
, there exists a projector
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
;

3.

For every projectors
\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}
, there exist an additive conjunction
\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}
as well as an additive disjunction
\mathcal{P}_{A}\cup\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}
;

4.

For every projectors
\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\mathcal{P}_{B}
, there exist a multiplicative conjunction
\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}
as well as a multiplicative disjunction
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}
;

5.

There are two constants
\left(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{D}\right)
that go with each additive binary connectors;

6.

There are two constants
\left(1,1\right)
that go with each multiplicative binary connectors.

And the properties that can be proven for the projectors are similar to those that can be proven in MALL using the sequent calculus:

1.

All binary connectors are commutative;

2.

Multiplicative connectors distribute over additive ones;

3.

All propositions have a negation obeying the De Morgan rules (
39
);

4.

Additive constants are the negation of each other;

5.

Multiplicative constants are the negation of each other.

Proposition 1 is always true as we take it as an axiom.
Propositions 2 to 4 are true from the fact that the definition of these various connectors implies closure, which, in the case of projectors, is the conservation of properties (
80
). These were proven for each connector in the previous sections.
Proposition 5 follows from equations (
102
) and (
105
).
Proposition 6 happens because of the isomorphism
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}\right)\otimes\mathbb{C}\cong 1
so that
\mathcal{P}\otimes 1=\mathcal{P}
and the same way,
\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}\otimes\overline{1}}=\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}}=\mathcal{P}
,
\overline{1\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}}=\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}}=\mathcal{P}
.

Property 1 is true from the definition. In the case of the mutiplicative connectors,
\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}
and
\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}
, the isomorphism
\mathcal{H}^{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B}\cong\mathcal{H}^{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A}
should of course be used.

Property 2 follows from Eqs. (
118
) and (
119
) in the case of
\otimes
and application of the De Morgan rules (
115
) on these two equations can be used to prove the property in the case of
\overline{\>\cdot\>}\rightarrow\>\cdot\>
. Put differently, the multiplicative conjunction (respectively, disjunction) distributes over the additive conjunction (disjunction)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)\>;

(147a)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\cup\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)=\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cup\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)\>.

(147b)

But as
\otimes
and
\rightarrow
are both operations that merge subspaces, the converse obviously does not hold. Indeed an expression like
\mathcal{P}_{X}\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)=\left(\mathcal{P}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{A}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)
makes no sense as the right-hand side feature a cap between two superoperator projectors defined on different spaces that are not isomorphic in general.

Property 3 was proven at Eq. (
111
) for the negation, Eq. (
115
) for the additive connectors, and follows from the definition in the case of multiplicative connectors.

Property 4 is the statement
\overline{\mathcal{I}}=\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}
, whose converse hold by idempotency or can be proven by the same kind of computation.

Property 5 is the statement
\overline{1}=1-1+1
, as in the 1-dimensional case
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{I}=1
.

There are however some discrepancies with MALL, that we now discuss. First, two small issues that indicate a departure from a faithful model of MALL as in Ref.
Girard 1987
:

1.

The multiplicative units are equivalent;

2.

The additive falsity (i.e. the unit for
\cup
) is not absorbant.

The first issue is why we use the terminology ‘degenerate’ in the main text to refer to the model of MALL formed by the algebra of projectors. The second issue –that
\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\neq\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}
– is more severe as it goes against an equality that can be proven in MALL. A way to circumvent this is to redefine the additive falsity as the number 0, but then the trace normalization of all operator systems the projectors characterize should be set to zero which would jeopardize the probabilistic interpretation.

A more substantial problem is the fact that the cap and cup are only well-defined for expression featuring the same set of base projectors: two projectors can only be composed with a cap if they can be embedded in the same space. This is part of the problem we evoked when discussing Property 2 above. For the model of logic to work properly, the number of subsystems as well as the dimension of each subsystem must be fixed
a priori
, and each expression is associated with a given (set of) subsystem(s), so that the composition is well-defined. Knowing this information allows one to ‘pad’ the expressions with identities so as to embed them in the global Hilbert space. For example, an expression like
\mathcal{P}_{X}\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)
can be made definite by knowing for example that there are two systems
A
and
B
, and that
\mathcal{P}_{X}
is associated with system
B
so that the padding reads
\mathcal{P}_{X}\cong\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{X}_{B}
, where
\mathcal{P}^{X}_{B}
is the projector
\mathcal{P}_{X}
acting on system
B
. Another possibility is that
\mathcal{P}_{X}
is associated with both systems, so that the padding is trivial
\mathcal{P}_{X}\cong\mathcal{P}^{X}_{AB}
. Yet another possibility is that the projector is associated with system
A
and
B
, but the global Hilbert space is tripartite:
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{C}
. In that case, the padding should be done on the two projectors:
\mathcal{P}_{X}\cong\mathcal{P}^{X}_{AB}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{C}
,
\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\cong\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{C}
.

Despite these particularities, the connection between the algebra of projectors and MALL is too obvious not to be pointed out.

Remark that the issues with interpreting the algebra as a model of LL come from the choice of additive connectives as
\cap
and
\cup
. This choice is motivated because we want to compare the underlying operator systems associated with different types of higher-order quantum transformations. Another choice proposed in Ref.
Simmons and Kissinger 2022
is to use the Cartesian product and direct sum as the additive conjunction and disjunction respectively. In that case, the issues can all be alleviated except for the degeneracy of the multiplicative units… but the additives connectors can no longer be used for comparison.

A.5
Adding the prec

Define the
A-to-B one-way signaling composition
, nicknamed
prec
, as

\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\equiv\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}-\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\>.

(148)

The reversed sign
\succ
(also nicknamed the prec) can be defined accordingly: the
B-to-A one-way signaling composition
is given by

\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\equiv\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}-\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\>.

(149)

As is the case with the transformation
\rightarrow
, note that
\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\cong\mathcal{P}_{B}\prec\mathcal{P}_{A}
since
\mathcal{H}^{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B}\cong\mathcal{H}^{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A}
.

Prec yields a valid projector on operator system:

When introduced in the main text, Eq. (
46
), this projector was obtained as the intersection of the following two projectors:

\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\equiv\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\>.

(150)

Because
\mathcal{I}_{A},\mathcal{P}_{A},\mathcal{P}_{B}
are valid projectors on operators systems, meaning they obey Eqs. (
80
), and we proved that operations
\overline{\>\cdot\>},\otimes
and
\cap
preserve this property in the last sections,
\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}
obeys Eqs. (
80
) as well and it is therefore a valid projector on an operator system in
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B}\right)
.

Prec preserves commutation:

For the same reason as above, if
\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}=\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}_{A}
and
\mathcal{P}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}=\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}_{B}
, then

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)=\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\>.

(151)

It also commutes with the B-to-A one-way signaling composition:

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)=\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\>,

(152)

again because

\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\equiv\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}-\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\;,

(153)

is an expression derived from connectives that preserve commutation:

\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\equiv\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}\right)\>.

(154)

Proving (
152
) thus reduces to prove the commutation of the terms in parenthesis in
\left(\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\right)\cap\left(\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}\right)\right)
, which follows from the associativity of the
\cap
and the commutation of the
\rightarrow
with the
\otimes
. The same way,

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)=\left(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\>.

(155)

A.5.1
The prec is associative and commutes with the negation

Compared to transformation, one-way signaling composition is better behaved in the sense that it is associative

\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\prec\mathcal{P}_{C}\right)=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\prec\mathcal{P}_{C}\>.

(156)

So that
\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\prec\mathcal{P}_{C}\right)=\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\prec\mathcal{P}_{C}
can be written unambiguously.
Even more, it is better behaved than both the no signaling (the tensor) and the two-way signaling (the transformation) compositions as it is distributive with respect to the negation

\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\prec\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}\>.

(157)

We first prove the distributivity of the negation by direct computation,

\overline{\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}}=\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}-\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}=\\
\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}-\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}+\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}-\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}=\\
\big(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}-\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}+\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\big)-\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}=\\
\big(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}\big)-\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}=\\
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\prec\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}\>.

(158)

This can be used to prove associativity,

\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\prec\mathcal{P}_{C}\right)=\\
\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\prec\mathcal{P}_{C}\right)-\\
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{C}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{C}=\\
\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{C}-\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{C}+\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{C}-\\
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{C}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{C}=\\
\big(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}\big)\otimes\mathcal{P}_{C}-\big(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}-\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\big)\otimes\mathcal{D}_{C}+\\
\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{C}=\\
\big(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}\big)\otimes\mathcal{P}_{C}-\big(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\prec\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}\big)\otimes\mathcal{D}_{C}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{C}=\\
\big(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}\big)\otimes\mathcal{P}_{C}-\overline{\big(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\big)}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{C}+\left(\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\right)\otimes\mathcal{D}_{C}=\\
\big(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\big)\prec\mathcal{P}_{C}\>,

(159)

where we have used the definition to go to the last line, and commutation of the prec with the negation to go to the penultimate one.

A.5.2
Inclusion relations of the prec

In Sec.
A.4.1
, it was shown that the tensor product of two projectors defines a subset of the composition of the same two projectors using the transformation connective seen as a composition, proving Eq. (
33
) of the main text. That is,
\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}=\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}
, see the discussion around Eq. (
131
).
As the prec is itself a form of composition as argued in Sec.
V.1
, similar inclusion relations can be proven.
Like the tensor product, one-way signaling composition with the trivial system amounts to doing nothing since
\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec 1=\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes 1-\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes 1+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes 1=\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}}\otimes 1
and
1\prec\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes 1-\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes 1+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes 1
so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec 1=\mathcal{P}_{A}\>,

(160a)

\displaystyle 1\prec\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A}\>.

(160b)

One can link the two-way signaling, one-way signaling, and no signaling compositions, respectively,
\overline{\>\cdot\>}\rightarrow\cdot
,
\cdot\prec\cdot
, and
\cdot\otimes\cdot
, by noticing that

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)=\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\>,

(161)

and

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)=\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\>.

(162)

Negating
\mathcal{P}_{A}
yields relations (
52
) in the main text.
Notice that the input of the transformation has to be negated to be interpreted as a composition because of how it was defined in Ref.
Perinotti 2017
: it
transforms
an
input
in
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)
to an output in
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{B}\right)
, hence it must be a functional on
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)
, meaning it should locally belong to
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
instead of
\mathscr{A}
(see the discussion in Sec.
IV.2
).
On the contrary, the tensor and the prec
compose
an
output
in
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{A}\right)
with an output in
\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{B}\right)
.

The first equation is proven by developing it,

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)=\big(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}-\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\big)\cap\big(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}-\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\big)
,
and noting that the intersection of any two elements but
\big(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\big)\cap\big(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}\big)=\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}
is giving
\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}
. Thus, the expression reduces to
\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}
followed by eight occurrences of
\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}
alternating between a plus and minus sign, therefore canceling each other.
The second equation has a quick proof using the De Morgan rule and the distributivity of the negation:

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:invol}}{=}\overline{\overline{\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cup\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)}}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:deMorgan_cup_to_cap}}{=}\overline{\overline{\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)}\cap\overline{\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)}}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:semi-causal_negation}}{=}\overline{\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\prec\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\succ\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}\right)}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:causal=0ways}}{=}\overline{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{B}}=\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\>.

(163)

From Eq. (
161
), it can also be inferred that

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\>,

(164a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\>,

(164b)

as, obviously,
\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)=\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}
, and the second equation is proven analogously. The same way, from Eq. (
162
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\>,

(165a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\>.

(165b)

Putting these relations together,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\>,

(166a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{A}\succ\mathcal{P}_{B}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\>.

(166b)

Figure 9:
Diagram of the subspaces defined by the three ways of defining a composite projector.

The first line is diagrammatically depicted in Fig.
9
: the subspace associated with
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}
is represented in blue on the right. The intermediate case, associated with
\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}
is depicted in green on the centre; from Eq. (
46
), the green part,
\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\equiv\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)
, is obtained as the intersection of the blue part,
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}
, with
\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}=\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cup\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)
which correspond to the right and top quadrants. It indeed recovers the other definition (
148
),
\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\equiv\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}-\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}
: the green part made of the right and top quadrants (
\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}
) minus the line separating the left and top quadrants (
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}
) except at the central dot (
\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}
).
The smallest case, associated with
\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}
is depicted in yellow on the left; as discussed in Corollary
2
it is effectively defined by the intersection of no signaling subspaces of Lemma
5
,
\left(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}\right)\cap\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}\right)
, that is the intersection of the right and top quadrants (
\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{B}
) with the right and bottom ones (
\mathcal{P}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}
); the intersection with
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{B}
brings no new constraints.

Note that similar diagrams appear in Figs.
7
and
10
, but they once again correspond to the situation where
\mathcal{P}_{A}
is negated. Negating
\mathcal{P}_{A}
in Eqs. (
166
) indeed yields the relations (
56
) of the main text depicted in these figures.

A.5.3
Distribution properties of the prec

Cap and prec satisfy an interchange law,

\big(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\big)\prec\big(\mathcal{P}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\big)=\big(\mathcal{P}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}_{B}\big)\cap\big(\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\prec\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\big)\>.

(167)

This can be shown as follows:

\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right)\prec\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)\\
=\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)-\overline{\left(\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}\right)}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:deMorgan_cap_to_cup}}{=}\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\left(\mathcal{P}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\right)-\left(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\cup\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\right)\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\\
=\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\big(\mathcal{P}_{B}\cap\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{B}\big)-\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}-\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\\
+\big(\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{A}\cap\overline{\mathcal{P}^{{}^{\prime}}_{A}}\big)\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}+\mathcal{D}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B}\\
